Gideon foretells the conception, and David tells of thy childbirth, O Theotokos: for the Word descended upon thy womb as rain upon the fleece, and without seed didst thou sprout forth, O holy earth, the salvation of the world, Christ our God, O full of grace!

*At the end of Matins:*
The Son and Word of God, begotten of the Father before the ages without a mother...

*Tone 7:*
*On Sunday evening, and on Monday at "God is the Lord":*
O immaculate Virgin Theotokos, entreat thy Son together with the heavenly hosts, that forgiveness of transgressions may be granted us before the end, who faithfully glorify thee.

*At the end of Matins:*
Thou hast surpassed the heavenly hosts, being shown forth as an edifice, a divine temple, O blessed Theotokos, for thou didst give birth to Christ, the Savior of our souls.

*On Monday evening, and on Tuesday at "God is the Lord":*
Rejoice, offered unto thee, O Theotokos O Theotokos, for thou wast shown forth higher than the angels, having given birth to God.

*At the end of Matins:*
Pacify our life by the prayers of the Theotokos, as we cry unto Thee: O Merciful Lord, glory to Thee!

*On Tuesday evening, and on Wednesday at "God is the Lord":*
Christ God Who was crucified for us, and destroyed the dominion of death, unceasingly entreat, O Virgin Theotokos, that He may save our souls.

*At the end of Matins:*
Deliver us, O Theotokos, from the sins that hold us fast, for we the faithful have no other hope save thee, and God Who was born of thee.

*On Wednesday evening, and on Thursday at "God is the Lord":*
The fruit of thy womb, O all-pure one, is the fulfillment of the prophets and the Law: therefore, glorifying thee in knowledge as Theotokos, we piously magnify thee.

*At the end of Matins:*
The fruit of thy womb, O Bride of God, was shown forth as mediating salvation for men. Therefore, glorifying thee as Theotokos in mind and tongue, we the faithful magnify thee.

*On Thursday evening, and on Friday at "God is the Lord":*
Christ God Who was crucified for us...

*At the end of Matins:*
Deliver us, O Theotokos...

*On Friday evening, and on Saturday at "God is the Lord":*
As the treasury of our resurrection, O all-hymned one, lead up from the pit and the abyss of transgressions those who hope in thee; for thou hast saved those guilty of sin, having given birth to our Salvation: who before childbirth wast a Virgin, and in childbirth a Virgin, and after childbirth still remainest a Virgin.

*At the end of Matins:*
Rejoice, thou who in thy womb didst contain Him Whom the heavens cannot contain! Rejoice, O Virgin, proclamation of the prophets, through whom Emmanuel shone forth! Rejoice, Mother of Christ God!

*Tone 8:*
*On Sunday evening:*
Rejoice, thou who through the angel didst receive the joy of the world! Rejoice, thou who gavest birth to thy Creator and Lord! Rejoice, thou who wast deemed worthy to be the Mother of God!

*At the end of Matins:* [—][^230]

*And on Monday at "God is the Lord":*
Rejoice, gate of the King of glory, which the Most High alone passed through, and preserved sealed alone, for the salvation of our souls.

*On Monday evening, and on Tuesday at "God is the Lord": On Monday evening, and on Tuesday at "God is the Lord":*
The immovable confirmation of the faith, and the precious gift of our souls, the Theotokos do we the faithful magnify with songs: Rejoice, thou who didst contain in thy womb the Rock of life! Rejoice, hope of the ends [of the earth], protection of the sorrowing! Rejoice, O unwedded Bride!

*At the end of Matins:*
O all-pure Virgin, save us by thy prayers, moving thy maternal compassion toward thy Son and our God.

*On Tuesday evening, and on Wednesday at "God is the Lord":*
Beholding upon the Cross the Lamb, and Shepherd, and Savior of the world, she that gave Thee birth said weeping: "The world indeed rejoices, receiving redemption, but my womb burns, beholding Thy crucifixion, which Thou endurest for all, O my Son and my God!"

*At the end of Matins:*
The fruit of thy womb, O all-pure one, is the fulfillment of the prophets and the Law: therefore, glorifying thee in knowledge as Theotokos, we piously magnify thee.

*On Wednesday evening, and on Thursday at "God is the Lord":*
O all-pure Theotokos, spiritual gate of life, deliver from perils those who faithfully flee unto thee, that we may glorify thy most holy childbirth for the salvation of our souls.

*At the end of Matins:*
O all-pure Virgin, save us...

*On Thursday evening, and on Friday at "God is the Lord":*
The Lamb, and Shepherd...

*At the end of Matins:*
The fruit of thy womb, O all-pure one...

*On Friday evening, and on Saturday at "God is the Lord":*
O Good One, Who for our sake wast born of a Virgin, and didst endure crucifixion, Who by death didst overthrow death, and manifested the resurrection as God: despise not those whom Thou hast fashioned with Thine own hand. Show Thy love for mankind, O Merciful One. Accept the Theotokos who gave Thee birth, who prays for us, and save, O our Savior, a despairing people.

*At the end of Matins:*
O all-pure Virgin, save us by thy prayers, moving thy maternal compassion toward thy Son and our God.

*End of the Theotokia.*

### Notes

[^5]: Slavonic: ржⷭ҇тво̀ (childbearing / nativity), referring here to the Mother of God's bringing forth of the incarnate Word, or to her childbearing.
[^6]: Slavonic: дв҃е (O virgin / maiden); used here poetically of St. Mary of Egypt as an undefiled bride of Christ through repentance.
[^7]: An allusion to the Canticle of Symeon the God-receiver (Luke 2:29).
[^8]: St. Titus the Wonderworker (9th c.), Studite monk and confessor during the second Iconoclastic controversy under Leo V the Armenian.
[^9]: Slavonic: Монашествꙋ́ющихъ сто́лпъ да пое́тся Ті́тъ. Acrostic across the troparia of the canon of St. Titus.
[^10]: St. Nicetas the Confessor (c. 760–824), Hegumen of the Medikion Monastery in Bithynia, an ardent defender of the veneration of holy icons under the Iconoclastic emperors Leo V the Armenian and Michael II.
[^11]: The catchword at the bottom of Fol. 6r reads 'Ст҃а́ѧ' (possibly anticipating an unprinted sticheron or rubric), whereas the first word on Fol. 6v begins 'Єгда̀ во и҆згна́нїихъ'.
[^12]: Slavonic acrostic: Твоѐ чисто́ю всесвѣ́тлое житїѐ ѻ҆́тче.
[^13]: Slavonic: дольрѣ́тныѧ сла́сти (an anomalous printed form for earthly/lowly pleasures, likely representing an early East Slavic corruption of до́льнїѧ / долерѣ́чныя).
[^14]: The name Nicetas (Νικήτας) is derived from the Greek νίκη (victory).
[^15]: St. Joseph the Hymnographer (c. 816–886), prolific Byzantine melodist and poet; and St. George of Maleon (9th c.), monk and ascetic on Cape Maleas in the Peloponnese.
[^16]: Slavonic acrostic: Твоѧ̑ оу҆се́рднѡ ѽ ѻ҆́тче пою̀ болѣ̑зни.
[^17]: Sts. Theodulus the Lector and Agathopus the Deacon of Thessalonica (martyred under Maximian/Diocletian, c. 303), drowned in the sea with stones tied around their necks. In many Greek and post-Nikonian editions, this day also commemorates Sts. Claudius, Diodorus, Victor, Victorinus, Papias, Serapion, and Nicephorus; the 1761 Pochaiv Menaion here gives sole precedence to Theodulus, Agathopus, and their companions.
[^18]: Slavonic acrostic: Твои́хъ воспѣва́ю мꙋ́чениковъ хрⷭ҇тѐ красотꙋ̀. І҆ѡ́сифъ.
[^19]: The 'Levitical vesture' (леѵі́тскаѧ ѡ҆де́жда) and 'ministry of the first martyr' (первомꙋ́ченика слꙋ́жба) refer to the diaconal rank of St. Agathopus, patterned after St. Stephen the Protomartyr and Archdeacon.
[^20]: St. Eutychius, Patriarch of Constantinople (c. 512–582; commemorated April 6), presided over the Fifth Ecumenical Council (Constantinople II, 553), was exiled under Justinian for refusing Aphthartodocetism, and was later restored.
[^21]: Slavonic acrostic: Чꙋдодѣ́тельномꙋ є҆ѵтѵ́хїю оу҆дивла́юсѧ (in Greek: Τὸν θαυματουργὸν Εὐτύχιον θαυμάζω). The canon is authored by St. Theophanes the Branded (c. 778–845).
[^22]: The 1761 Pochaiv typesetter erroneously printed 'є҆го́-но́зе' across the leaf break (Fol. 16r–16v) for 'є҆сѝ мно́гаѧ' or 'мно́зѣ' (cf. standard Menaion: 'чꙋдодѣ́йствовалъ є҆сѝ мно́гаѧ и҆ и҆зрѧ́днаѧ').
[^23]: St. George the Confessor, Bishop of Mytilene on Lesbos (c. 776–821; commemorated April 7), defender of the holy icons against the iconoclasts under Leo the Armenian, died in exile in Chersonesus.
[^24]: The Holy Apostles Herodion, Agabus, Asyncritus, Rufus, Phlegon, and Hermes (and Hermas) of the Seventy are commemorated on April 8. Herodion was bishop of Patras, Rufus bishop of Thebes (in Greece), Agabus the prophet (Acts 11:28, 21:10), Asyncritus bishop of Hyrcania, Phlegon bishop of Marathon, and Hermes bishop of Dalmatia.
[^25]: Slavonic acrostic: А҆́плѡмъ неплете́мъ бж҃е́ственною пѣ́снь. Творе́нїе І҆ѡ́сифово (Unto the apostles we weave a divine song. The work of Joseph [the Hymnographer]).
[^26]: St. Herodion (Rodion), a kinsman of the Apostle Paul (cf. Rom. 16:11), was consecrated the first bishop of Patras in Achaea, where he suffered martyrdom.
[^27]: St. Phlegon, of the Seventy Apostles, was bishop of Marathon in Attica.
[^28]: St. Hermes, of the Seventy Apostles, was bishop of Dalmatia.
[^29]: St. Eupsychius of Caesarea in Cappadocia (martyred c. 362 under Julian the Apostate; commemorated April 9) destroyed the pagan temple of Fortune (Tyche), for which he was tortured and beheaded.
[^30]: Slavonic acrostic: Е҆ѵѱѵ́хїа бл҃годꙋ́шнѡ пою̀ болѣ́зни. Творе́нїе І҆ѡ́сифово (Courageously I sing the sufferings of Eupsychius. The work of Joseph [the Hymnographer]).
[^31]: The Holy Martyrs Terence, Pompeius, Africanus, Maximus, Macarius, and their 36 companions were martyred at Carthage in Africa during the Decian persecution (c. 250; commemorated April 10).
[^32]: Slavonic acrostic: Мно́жество мѧ̀ спасѝ добропобѣ́дныхъ, мꙋ́ченикѡвъ. Творе́нїе І҆ѡ́сифово (Save me, O multitude of fair-victorious martyrs. The work of Joseph [the Hymnographer]).
[^33]: The Holy Hieromartyr Antipas, Bishop of Pergamum in Asia, was a disciple of the Apostle John the Theologian (cf. Rev. 2:13) and was martyred by being cast into a red-hot bronze bull under Domitian (c. 92; commemorated April 11).
[^34]: Slavonic acrostic: Свѣ́тлаго въ мꙋ́ченицѣхъ А҆нти́пꙋ чествꙋ́ю. Творе́нїе І҆ѡ́сифово (I honor Antipas, radiant among martyrs. The work of Joseph [the Hymnographer]).
[^35]: Our Venerable Father Basil the Confessor, Bishop of Parium in Mysia (commemorated April 12), opposed the Iconoclasts during the eighth-century persecutions, enduring exile and tribulations for his defense of the holy icons.
[^36]: The Slavonic printed text reads лю́тагѡ сїѧ́нїа ("cruel shining"), an apparent typographical corruption for изгна́нїа ("exile"; Greek: πικρᾶς ἐξορίας).
[^37]: The Slavonic text reads Сїѧ́ твоѐ житїѐ ѻ҆́ч҃е преподо́бныхъ (with Сїѧ́ apparently a scribal error for сїѐ or сїѧ́ѧ, or omitting a verb such as препро́водилъ є҆сѝ; Greek: Ὁσίως διαγαγὼν τὸ ζῆν).
[^38]: The Holy Hieromartyr Artemon, Presbyter of Laodicea in Phrygia (commemorated April 13), ministered into extreme old age and was martyred under Diocletian (c. 303).
[^39]: In the Greek and Church Slavonic Menaion, the acrostic of the canon is alphabetical (Greek: Ἡ ἀκροστιχίς κατ᾽ ἀλφάβητον; Church Slavonic: По алфави́тꙋ), with the stanzas originally keyed to the letters of the Greek alphabet.
[^40]: Slavonic: стрѣлоно́сче ("arrow-bearer"; Greek: βελοφόρε), denoting one who bears the marks or darts of martyrdom for Christ.
[^41]: The printed Slavonic text reads ѡ҆кли́манъ (an obscure or corrupted reading, perhaps for оу҆молѝ ["entreat"] or ѡ҆клица́емъ ["we cry aloud unto"], or representing Greek ὦ κλεινέ ["O renowned one"]): "entreat Him, as He is tender-hearted."
[^42]: Saint Martin the Confessor, Pope of Rome (649–655, commemorated April 14), convened the Lateran Council of 649 in Rome to condemn Monothelitism, for which he was arrested by Emperor Constans II, brought to Constantinople, tortured, and exiled to Cherson in Taurica (Crimea), where he died in 655.
[^43]: The canon to Saint Martin the Confessor is the work of Joseph the Hymnographer, with the acrostic in the Greek Menaion: Μαρτῖνον ὑμνῶ τὸν σοφὸν Ῥώμης πρόεδρον. Ἰωσήφ (I hymn Martin, the wise prelate of Rome. Joseph).
[^44]: At the Lateran Council of 649, Pope Saint Martin I condemned the Monothelite leaders Pyrrhus, Sergius I, and Paul II of Constantinople, Theodore of Pharan, and Cyrus of Alexandria.
[^45]: The printed Slavonic text exhibits a dittography: пред̾ сꙋдѧ́щихъ сꙋдѧ́щихъ ("standing before the judges of judges" or repeated "of the judging").
[^46]: The Holy Apostles Aristarchus, Pudens, and Trophimus of the Seventy were disciples and traveling companions of the Apostle Paul (cf. Acts 19:29, 20:4, 27:2; Col. 4:10; Philem. 1:24; 2 Tim. 4:20-21). According to tradition, they were beheaded in Rome under Nero during the same persecution in which Saint Paul suffered martyrdom.
[^47]: The acrostic of the canon in the Greek Menaion is: Θείας μαθηταῖς θεῖον ἐξᾴδω μέλος. Ἰωσήφ ("Unto the divine disciples a divine song I chant. Joseph"), translated in Slavonic as: Бж҃їимъ оу҆ченикѡ́мъ бж҃твеннꙋю воспѣва́ю пѣ́снь. Творе́нїе Їѡ́сифово.
[^48]: Slavonic: і҆́дѡльскꙋю ве́щь попали́сте ("burned up the matter/stuff of idols"), corresponding to Greek ὕλην εἰδώλων.
[^49]: The Holy Virgin-Martyrs Agape, Irene, and Chionia were sisters from Aquileia (or Thessalonica) who suffered martyrdom under Diocletian (c. 304). After refusing to eat sacrificial meats and hiding sacred Christian books during the persecutions, Agape and Chionia were burned alive, while Irene was subsequently pierced by an arrow.
[^50]: The canon to the Holy Virgin-Martyrs Agape, Irene, and Chionia is attributed in the title to Joseph the Hymnographer (*Творе́нїе Їѡ́сифово*), Tone 8.
[^51]: The printed Slavonic text reads `дв҃ы, съ си̑мъ бл҃же́жатсѧ`, an apparent typographical corruption for `блажа́тсѧ` ("are called blessed", or `сла́вѧтсѧ`). In the Theotokion of Ode 1, the printed text has the abbreviation `бл҃гоче́.` (for `бг҃оневѣ́сто` or `влⷣчце`).
[^52]: Slavonic: `велехва́лный ѡ҆нѣмѣ̀` ("the boastful one was struck dumb"), referring to the persecuting Roman magistrate Dulcitius or the judge Sisinius.
[^53]: The printed Slavonic text reads `сла́ва та̀ ра́достнѡ` ("praising thee joyfully"), where `сла́ва` is an epigraphic error in the Pochaiv print for `сла́вѧ` (participial "glorifying/praising thee").
[^54]: Both Saint Symeon and Saint Acacius are titled in the printed rubric as `прпⷡднагѡ` ("our venerable father"). Saint Symeon of Persia was Bishop of Seleucia-Ctesiphon and was martyred by beheading under King Shapur II of Persia on Good Friday in 341 (or 344), along with over a thousand fellow Christians and clergy; he is therefore celebrated as a Hieromartyr. Saint Acacius was Bishop of Melitene in Armenia (5th century), an outspoken defender of Orthodoxy at the Third Ecumenical Council in Ephesus (431) against Nestorius, and renowned as a wonderworker.
[^55]: The printed Slavonic text reads `нѣ̑маѧ` ("speechless/mute things"), an apparent compositor error for `нб҃снаѧ` (*небе́снаѧ*, "heavenly things"), parallel to the antitheses: "earthly/heavenly, transient/enduring, corruptible/incorruptible" (cf. Greek τὰ οὐράνια τοῖς ἐπιγείοις).
[^56]: Slavonic: `посрами́въ свѣ́тство`, an epigraphic corruption in the Pochaiv print for `свирѣ́пство` ("ferocity/cruelty") or `совѣ́тъ` ("counsel/assembly"), referring to the deposition of Nestorius and the condemnation of his heresy at the Council of Ephesus.
[^57]: The acrostic in the Greek Menaion is: Στέφω σε τὸν μάκαρα Συμεὼν ᾄσμασιν. Ἰωσήφ ("I crown thee, the blessed Symeon, with songs. Joseph"), translated into Church Slavonic as: Вѣнча́ю блаже́ннаго тѧ̀ Сѷмеѡ́на пѣснопѣ́нїи. Творе́нїе І҆ѡ́сифово.
[^58]: Slavonic: `пре́лести мре́теченїа ... ста́до сла́вне`. The printed word `мре́теченїа` appears to be a typesetting corruption for `мꙋ́тнотече́нїѧ` ("turbid currents") or `моретече́нїѧ` ("surging currents of the sea").
[^59]: Slavonic: `Седо́мь свѧ́занъ`, an archaic form or typographical variant for `сѣди́нами свѧ́занъ` ("bound in gray-haired old age") or `сꙋдо́мъ` ("by the judgment/tribunal"); tradition records that Saint Symeon suffered martyrdom at an advanced age under King Shapur II.
[^60]: Saint Ananias was a presbyter and fellow martyr of Saint Symeon in Persia. The phrase `подвѣ́сныѧ ... мꙋ́ченики` refers to those martyrs who were suspended or hung up during their torture before their decapitation.
[^61]: According to the Life of Saint Acacius of Melitene, the river overflowing its banks threatened to flood the city. Acacius prayed, set a large stone by the riverbed as a boundary mark, and commanded the waters in Christ's name never to transgress that stone, whereupon the flood abated.
[^62]: The printed Slavonic reads `нена́гѡ бо цр҃ѧ позна̀` ("for he recognized [the servant] of the heavenly King"), where `нена́гѡ` is a contracted/abbreviated compositor form for `небе́снагѡ` (`нб҃снагѡ`). The troparion refers to the pious Byzantine emperor (Theodosius II) receiving Saint Acacius with deep reverence.
[^63]: Slavonic: `ѡ҆́брѣбаемое` (or `ѡ҆́брѣтаемое`), an epigraphic corruption for `ѡ҆бꙋрева́емое` ("storm-tossed, tempest-tossed").
[^64]: Slavonic: `по́дъ а҆ка́кїе` (or `впре́дъ` / `па́ки`). The troparion recalls the miracle in the Life of Saint Acacius where a swarm of biting flies or gnats plagued the sick; upon the saint's prayer and command, they vanished and never returned.
[^65]: Saint Symeon of Persia was martyred together with about a hundred fellow Christians, priests, and deacons (according to one tradition, 100 martyrs on Good Friday; in another expanded account, up to 1,000 or 1,150 during the week). The Slavonic term `сточи́сленное` translates the Greek ἑκατοντάριθμον ("hundred-numbered").
[^66]: The Life of Saint Acacius records that while he was praying near a marsh or river, the loud croaking of frogs disturbed the divine service. Acacius addressed the frogs, bidding them be silent and not interrupt prayer to God, and they remained mute.
[^67]: During a drought in Melitene, Saint Acacius prayed, and torrential rain fell, opening the heavens (like Elijah), and in another circumstance water miraculously gushed forth from dry rock.
[^68]: The single word *наслажде́нію* ('enjoyment') concludes the final troparion of Ode 9 of the canon to Saint Acacius of Melitene begun on fol. 44v (physical leaf p90).
[^69]: The Slavonic text reads: *Соприсносу́щное и҆ є҆ди́нородное сло́во бг҃а со ѻ҆тце́мъ и҆ дх҃омъ* ('Co-eternal and only-begotten Word of God with the Father and the Spirit'), where the printed leaf breaks *є҆ди́но-* across lines.
[^70]: The phrase *видѣ́нїемъ и҆ дѣ́янымъ любому́дрїемъ* translates the Greek ascetic hendiadys θεωρίᾳ καὶ πράξει ('by contemplation and practical ascetic action/philosophy').
[^71]: The Slavonic *я́кѡ гꙋ́ба и҆ налїя̀* renders the Greek ὡς σπόγγος καὶ ἀντλητήριον ('like a sponge and a vessel/ladle for drawing water'), signifying the saint's receptive absorption of the monastic virtues of Saint Chariton the Confessor.
[^72]: At the turn from fol. 48r to fol. 48v, the catchword printed at the foot of fol. 48r is *И҆ ла́дїю* ('And the bark/ship'), whereas the first troparion at the head of fol. 48v begins with *Наде́ждею я́же къ бг҃у* ('Strengthened by hope toward God'). This reflects an erratum in the Pochaiv pressroom's catchword setting.
[^73]: The Slavonic text *свяще́нникъ бы́въ пребл҃же́нне ст҃ителю* addresses Saint John of the Ancient Caves as *ст҃ителю* ('hierarch' or 'sacred celebrant'), reflecting his presbyteral/hierarchical dignity within the Lavra of Saint Chariton.
[^74]: The Slavonic text reads: *тя̀ мнѣ̀ сохранѝ*, where *мнѣ̀* appears as an ethical dative or an enclitic variant in the Pochaiv edition for *тя̀ невреди́ма сохранѝ* ('preserved thee unharmed') or *тя̀ соблюдѐ*.
[^75]: St. Theodore Trichinas (Theodore the Hairshirt-Wearer, d. c. 400), commemorated April 20, derived his epithet from the rough goat-hair garment (*trichinon*) he wore throughout his ascetic life in Thrace near Constantinople.
[^76]: The Staurotheotokion is prescribed in the Menaion when the commemoration occurs on a Wednesday or Friday during non-festal periods.
[^77]: An allegorical allusion to the carnal lusts and bondage of worldly passions, typified by Egypt in monastic ascetic literature.
[^78]: The Kontakion of St. Theodore Trichinas, sung in Tone 4 to the Podoben "Thou hast appeared today".
[^79]: An allusion to the Burning Bush of Sinai (Exodus 3:2), prefiguring the virgin birth of Christ without violation of virginity.
[^80]: The name Theodore (Greek: Θεόδωρος) translates literally as "Gift of God" (*theos* + *doron*), alluded to in the hymnographic text (`бж҃їѧ да́ра тезоимени́те`).
[^81]: St. Januarius (San Gennaro), bishop of Benevento, suffered martyrdom under Diocletian c. 305 with his companions. St. Theodore of Perge in Pamphylia was a soldier martyred under Antoninus Pius c. 138–161.
[^82]: The six companion martyrs of St. Januarius: the deacons Socius and Proculus, the lector Desiderius, Faustus, Eutychius, and Acutius.
[^83]: The Heirmos of Tone 4 Ode 1 invokes the Greek *tristatai* (Exodus 14:7, LXX: τριστάτας), the Egyptian chariot officers/captains drowned in the Red Sea, here allegorized as the tripartite faculties of the soul (*rational, irascible, concupiscent*).
[^84]: St. Januarius and his companions were thrown to wild beasts in the Flavian Amphitheatre at Pozzuoli, but the beasts refused to touch them, echoing Daniel in the lions' den (Daniel 6:16–22).
[^85]: Dioscorus, a soldier or prison guard who witnessed the miracles of St. Theodore of Perge, was converted to Christ and martyred by fire.
[^86]: According to the Acts of St. Januarius, when the judge Timotheus was struck with sudden blindness, the holy bishop Januarius prayed and restored his sight, yet the tyrant remained obstinate in his paganism.
[^87]: St. Socrates and St. Dionysius were soldiers who, witnessing the miraculous preservation of St. Theodore in the flames, confessed Christ and were cast into the furnace with him.
[^88]: Dioscorus, a soldier guarding St. Theodore of Perge, witnessed the saint's miracles and was converted, later suffering martyrdom by fire.
[^89]: The six companion martyrs of the holy Hieromartyr Januarius: the deacons Socius and Proculus, the lector Desiderius, Faustus, Eutychius, and Acutius.
[^90]: The printed Slavonic text reads 'И̓гла̀' (an apparent typographical error for 'И̓гда̀' / 'Єгда̀', meaning 'When').
[^91]: St. Philippa, the mother of St. Theodore of Perge. Note that the catchword at the foot of leaf 111 reads 'съ смѣ́хомъ' ('with laughter/joy'), while the top line of leaf 112 reads 'съ спѣ́хомъ' ('with haste').
[^92]: Church Slavonic 'непрее́мное' translates Greek ἀδιάδοχον, signifying the unending Kingdom that cannot be transferred to a successor.
[^93]: St. Theodore the Sykeote (d. 613), bishop of Anastasiopolis in Galatia, born in the village of Sykeon, celebrated for his ascetic wonders and prophetic gifts.
[^94]: The acrostic of the canon in the Greek Menaion is: Θεοῦ σε δῶρον οἶδα, Θεόδωρε μάκαρ. Ἰωσήφ ("I know thee as God's gift, blessed Theodore. Joseph").
[^95]: Church Slavonic 'ѡ̓даѧ́нїи' represents the painful ascetic mortifications and abstinences of the venerable Theodore.
[^96]: The name Theodore (Greek: Θεόδωρος) literally signifies "gift of God" (*Theos* + *doron*), here explicitly referenced by the hymnographer: 'Ѡ̓ бг҃а тезоимени́тъ да́ръ'.
[^97]: According to the Life of St. Theodore the Sykeote, the Holy Great-Martyr George appeared to him from childhood, guiding him to pray at his church and becoming his heavenly patron and constant companion.

[^177]: Isaiah 19:1: "Behold, the Lord rides upon a swift cloud, and shall come into Egypt: and the idols of Egypt shall be moved at his presence."
[^178]: Ode 5 Heirmos, Tone 1.
[^179]: Ode 6 Heirmos, Tone 1 (echoing Psalm 43:23 [LXX 43:22]).
[^180]: Reference to the prophecy of Isaiah 19:19: “In that day shall there be an altar to the Lord in the midst of the land of Egypt, and a pillar at the border thereof to the Lord.” The hymnographer poetically identifies the Holy Gospel according to Mark as this prophetic pillar in Egypt, where St. Mark founded the Church of Alexandria.
[^181]: The Slavonic rubric *судо́блаѧ* (*sudoblaya*) designates the appointed or prescribed festive ode designated by the Typikon for the Beatitudes, alternating with Ode 3 of the saint's canon.
[^182]: The printed Pochaiv text reads *ст҃ль мѣ́илсѧ є҆сѝ*, an evident typographical error or defective ligature for *свѧти́тель ꙗ҆ви́лсѧ є҆сѝ* (“thou didst appear as a hierarch”). Translated according to the clear sense of the office.
[^183]: The Pochaiv typesetter set both *сба́ти* (an erroneous partial typesetting of *сбавити* or *смягчити*) and its intended correction *оу҆ма́лити* (“to diminish”) directly side by side in the printed text (*де́рзость сба́ти оу҆ма́лити*). The redundant struck word *сбати* is omitted in translation.
[^184]: The printed text omits the initial letter *Ꙗ* in the podoben incipit (*Вї́лся* for *Ꙗ҆ви́лся є҆сѝ дне́сь* / “Thou hast appeared today”).
[^185]: According to the Life of St. Basil, Bishop of Amasea (martyred under Emperor Licinius, c. 322), after the saint was beheaded, his severed head and body were thrown separately into the Black Sea. By divine providence, both were washed ashore at Sinope, where fishermen discovered that the head had marvelously rejoined the neck with only a crimson line marking the stroke of the sword.
[^186]: St. Symeon, son of Cleopas (the brother of St. Joseph the Betrothed), succeeded St. James the Brother of the Lord as the second Bishop of Jerusalem. In extreme old age (traditionally 120 years), during the reign of Trajan, he was tortured and crucified, thus sharing the manner of Christ’s own Passion as celebrated throughout this office.
[^187]: St. Symeon of Jerusalem was martyred by crucifixion under the Roman proconsul Atticus in c. 106/107 AD; the hymnographer constantly highlights this direct physical imitation of the Passion of Christ on the Tree of the Cross.
[^188]: The printed Pochaiv text reads *стра́ство* (*strastvo*), an evident typographical conflation or dialectal form for *страда́льчество* (the state of suffering/martyrdom) or *ста́рчество* (venerable old age, referring to St. Symeon’s martyrdom at age 120).
[^189]: The printed text reads *стрѣ́пе* (*strepe*), a regional Ruthenian variant or misprint for *тре́пете* (*trepete*, "terror/trembling"), hence "terror of demons."
[^190]: The hymnographer plays upon the dual apostolic naming of the saint as both Simon and Symeon (*Сїмѡ́нъ сѷмеѡ́нъ*), mirroring his orthodox confession of the two natures (divine and human) of the incarnate Word against the early heretics.
[^191]: The Slavonic text reads *то́чы* (*tochy*), an archaic/Ruthenian nominal form corresponding to streams, gouts, or copious outflows (*то́ки*).
[^192]: Slavonic *блѧде́нїѧ* (*blyadeniya*), deriving from the Patristic root denoting senseless prating, ravings, delirium, or blasphemous pagan taunts.
[^193]: The Pochaiv printed text has the typographical error *по дꙋшелоби́тельномꙋ* (*po dushelobitel'nomu*) for *по дꙋшегꙋби́тельномꙋ* (*po dushegubitel'nomu*, “upon the soul-destroying path”).
[^194]: Slavonic *ѻ҆све́тней* (*osvetney*) in the printed text is an epigraphic defect/variant, likely intended for *всесвѣ́тней* (“all-worldly”) or *свѣ́тней* (“worldly/secular”) delusion.
[^195]: The printed Pochaiv text reads *Глы̑* (*Gly*) with circumflex, an evident printer’s defect or broken type for *Хꙋлы̑* (*Khuly*, “blasphemies/insults”) or *Злы̑* (*Zly*, “evil things”), referring to the martyrs despising the blasphemous insults or evils of the impious tormentors.
[^196]: In the printed heading, the commemoration is given as "Ст҃ы́хъ десѧтѝ мч҃никѡвъ" (*Of the Holy Ten Martyrs*), though the Menaion hymnography and traditional hagiography commemorate the Nine Martyrs of Cyzicus (Theognis, Rufus, Antipater, Theostichus, Artemas, Magnus, Theodotus, Thaumasius, and Philemon).
[^197]: Slavonic *Дїости́ха* (Theostichus / Diostichus), one of the Nine Martyrs of Cyzicus, named alongside Rufus and Antipater.
[^198]: Slavonic *Девѧтѝ мч҃нкѡвъ кра́снїи, по́лкъ сщ҃енныи* (*The fair regiment of the nine martyrs*), directly preserving the ninefold number contrary to the printed numeral in the heading.
[^199]: Ode 1 Heirmos: Slavonic *Трїста́ты крѣ́пкїѧ...* echoing Exodus 15:4 (Greek τριστάτας, captains or chariot officers).
[^200]: Slavonic *во́днагѡ трꙋ́да* (dropsy / edema, from Greek ὑδεριῶντα).
[^201]: Slavonic *къ чт҃ны̑мъ ковче́гѡмъ* (*unto your honorable shrines / reliquaries*).
[^202]: Kontakion, Tone 2: Slavonic *мꙋ́ченикъ девѧтѝ* (*of the nine martyrs*), maintaining the canonical ninefold count of the Cyzicus martyrs.
[^203]: Slavonic *на сꙋди́щи томꙋ̀ нача́льнѡ взыва́хꙋ* (at the tribunal they cried out unto the judge/ruler).
[^204]: Ode 9 troparion: In this concluding ode, the hymnographer explicitly names all nine martyrs of Cyzicus: Antipater, Theostichus, Rufus, Philemon, Thaumasius, Magnus, Artemas, Theognis, and Theodotus.
[^205]: The three readings at Vespers for the Feast of the Holy Apostle James are taken from the General Epistle of James: James 1:1–12; James 1:13–21a; and James 2:1–13.
[^206]: Slavonic *вѣ́ренъ бы́ти* ("to be faithful/devout"), rendering Greek θρῆσκος εἶναι (James 1:26).
[^207]: The Slavonic text explicitly prints *Їсма́ила всескве́рнаго*, though the biblical paradigm of selling the birthright and losing the paternal blessing on account of the belly refers to Esau (Genesis 25:29–34, 27). The printed reading is translated strictly as inked.
[^208]: Alluding to Luke 9:54–55, where James and John, the sons of Zebedee, asked whether they should command fire to come down from heaven and consume the inhospitable Samaritans, and Christ rebuked them.
[^209]: Alluding to Matthew 20:20–23 and Mark 10:35–40, where the sons of Zebedee sought to sit on the right and left hands of Christ in His kingdom.
[^210]: Alluding to Christ taking Peter, James, and John into the Garden of Gethsemane on the night of His betrayal (Matthew 26:37; Mark 14:33).
[^211]: Slavonic *а҆гарѧ́ны* (Hagarenes, i.e., Muslims / Tatars / Ottomans), reflecting the historical defense of Christian captives and holy shrines preserved in the Ruthenian liturgical recension.
[^212]: Alluding to Christ's words in Matthew 20:22–23: "Ye shall drink indeed of my cup, and be baptized with the baptism that I am baptized with."
[^213]: Alluding to Christ naming James and John *Boanerges*, that is, "Sons of thunder" (Mark 3:17).
[^214]: Recording the martyrdom of St. James the son of Zebedee by the sword under Herod Agrippa I (Acts 12:1–2).
[^215]: Alluding to Acts 12:21–23, where Herod Agrippa, having struck down James and sought to vex the Church, was smitten by an angel of the Lord and eaten of worms.
[^216]: The hymn repeats the text from the Aposticha on leaf p183, preserving the printed reading *Їсма́ила всескве́рнаго*.
[^217]: Liturgical pericopes for the Apostle: Epistle from Acts 12:1–11 (pericope 24); Gospel from Luke 5:1–11 (pericope 17, the calling of the first disciples, Peter, James, and John).
[^218]: The standard collection of Dogmatika (the Theotokia sung at "Lord, I have cried" on Saturday Vespers) and Aposticha Theotokia for the Eight Tones from the Octoechos, appended to the Menaion for use when a saint's office calls for the Resurrectional Theotokion of the tone.
[^219]: Psalm 44:10 (LXX Ps. 44:9): "The queen stood at Thy right hand, in apparel gilded and embroidered."
[^220]: This second appendix provides the weekday Dismissal and Aposticha Theotokia for each day of the week across the Eight Tones, appointed to be chanted when the Saint's office in the Menaion provides a "Glory" sticheron or sessional hymn in a particular tone, followed by "Both now" of the same tone according to the day of the week.
[^221]: Psalm 64:5 (LXX Ps. 64:4): "Holy is Thy temple, marvelous in righteousness."
[^222]: Reaffirming iconodulic orthodoxy and the veneration of the holy icon of the Mother of God against iconoclasts and heretics.
[^223]: Alluding to Daniel 7:9–10 and Matthew 25:31.
[^224]: A Hymn for the Departed (Поко́инъ) / mortuary Theotokion for the Friday evening commemoration of all departed faithful.
[^225]: Saturday Matins mortuary intercession for the departed.
[^226]: Slavonic *Не́бо пою́тъ тѧ̀* (attested in the Pochaiv print with *покꙋ́тъ*, an epigraphic/compositor error for *пою́тъ*), translated according to the intended liturgical reading.
[^227]: The Dismissal Theotokia (Apolytikia Theotokia) of the Eight Tones are sung throughout the liturgical year at daily Vespers, Matins, and Divine Liturgy.
[^228]: These Dismissal Theotokia (Bogorodichny Otpustytelny) are appointed to follow the troparia of the Saints throughout the liturgical year when the Menaion provides no dedicated Theotokion.
[^229]: The rubric appoints the general dismissal troparia to all saints and for the departed ('Apostles, martyrs, and prophets...' and 'Remember, O Lord, as Thou art good...') when Alleluia is sung at daily Matins (as during Great Lent and penitential seasons) in place of 'God is the Lord'.
[^230]: In the printed Slavonic text at the foot of folio 104r (p. 209), the rubric '*At the end of Matins:*' is printed without an accompanying hymn or incipit, immediately preceding the rubric for Monday at 'God is the Lord'. In standard liturgical practice, the sessional dismissal 'O all-pure Virgin, save us by thy prayers...' (printed on fol. 104v) is appointed here.
[^231]: The lower (rear) cover of the volume is bound in contemporary full brown calfskin over pasteboard, framed with blind-tooled double fillets and an inner ornamental roll border along the perimeter. No printed, manuscript, or stamped lettering is present on the outer leather.
[^232]: Photographic view of the untrimmed fore-edge of the closed codex text block supported in an archival conservation cradle, showing natural paper age-toning and the protruding edges of the leather binding boards.
[^233]: The lower pastedown is overlaid with a heavy paper repair sheet bearing a 20th-century Ukrainian archival accession number inscribed in graphite pencil: (*стд 172/4, інв. 354*), representing the call number of the codex in the holding library repository.
[^234]: Rear archival flyleaf recto; an unprinted blank handmade endpaper sheet exhibiting pronounced vertical folding creases, corner tears, and linen/hemp cord attachments along the inner gutter.
[^235]: Rear archival flyleaf verso; unprinted blank paper leaf facing the rear binding structure.
[^236]: The upper (front) cover features full brown calfskin over pasteboard with blind-tooled border fillets and an elaborate center stamped diamond-shaped cartouche composed of foliate corner flourishes and floral rosettes.
[^237]: The upper front pastedown preserves an exposed fragment of binder's waste (maculature) with recycled letterpress printing in German Fraktur typography (*...mein...*, *der*) used as reinforcement beneath the pastedown paper.
[^238]: Front archival flyleaf recto; an unprinted blank endpaper sheet of heavy, creased laid paper exhibiting binding cloth and cord fragments along the spine hinge.
[^239]: Front archival flyleaf verso; an unprinted blank paper leaf, with the inner gutter margin partially revealing the decorative architectural woodcut title border of the opening printed folio (*leaf p1*).
[^240]: Photographic view of the closed codex text block from the head-edge (or tail-edge) supported in conservation cradle brackets, displaying trimmed paper block edges and binding structure.
[^241]: Spine view of the volume bound in contemporary full brown calfskin with five raised bands forming six compartments, each decorated with blind-tooled foliate fleurons and roll tooling on the ribs. The uppermost compartment bears a 20th-century white paper library label inscribed by hand in ink and pencil: (*МІНЕЯ / Почаїв 1761 / березень квітень / Стд 172/4 (інв. 354)*), identifying the book as the 1761 Pochaiv Menaion for April (with a cancelled pencil attribution to March) under library inventory number 354.
[^242]: Photographic view of the untrimmed, age-toned tail-edge (bottom edge) of the closed codex text block supported in archival conservation foam rests, displaying the thick pasteboards, calfskin cover turn-ins, and lower endband structure.
